LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

* States Are Playing Serious Games on Virtual Battlefields
  Foreign Policy | 20 Nov 20:00 UTC
  The rise of video gaming has created new geopolitical arenas.
  FULL TEXT: The biggest single media event of the decade took place on Thursday, and most of Washington missed it. Grand Theft Auto VI , the latest installment of the most popular video game series in the world, racked up nearly $1.5 billion in sales even before it launched, already making back what may have been a more-than-a-billion dollar budget. In the next week, total sales are projected to hit over $3 billion. The previous game in the series still makes half a billion dollars a year through its online version alone. (For comparison purposes, Taylor Swift’s Eras tour, the biggest concert tour of all time, took in just over $2 billion, while Avengers: Endgame grossed roughly $2.9 billion.) Video games are cultural juggernauts , and governments have noticed. As industry consultant George Osborn points out in his new book, Power Play: Video Games, Politics, and the Battle for Global Influence , video gaming, once a solitary activity, has become a largely communal one. Online play creates a “third place” where people, especially the young, can mingle. As Osborn puts it, “what happens in video games communities bleeds out into reality—all the way into our political discourse.” The biggest single media event of the decade took place on Thursday, and most of Washington missed it.   FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Why have recent U.S. presidents done such a poor job of thinking about the consequences of wars before they start them? And is it possible that rather than learning lessons from the past, our leaders are actually getting worse at this critical task—more impulsive, less thoughtful—while Congress and the public let them get away with it? These are questions that run like a baleful subplot through Secrets , the new memoir from Bob Woodward, the Washington Post investigative reporter who, by delivering stunning scoops through 11 presidencies over a half century, has become a Washington institution in his own right. In his new book, he reveals for the first time the many deep-background sources on which he based his reporting over the decades. Woodward, of course, became famous as one half of the reporting duo that broke open the Watergate story (the other being Carl Bernstein), prompting the 1974 resignation of President Richard Nixon. Since then, he’s produced books on everything from the Supreme Court ( The Brethren ) to the doomed comedian John Belushi. But starting with his 1991 book, The Commanders , Woodward has spent a good part of his later career exploring what he calls “the most serious action a president can take”: declaring war. Much has been written about President Donald Trump’s ill-justified war against Iran that began earlier this year, and whether his controversial defense secretary, Pete Hegseth, should bear the blame for giving Trump far-too-optimistic assurances of success. * Pop Culture Still Can’t Figure Out Drone Warfare
  Foreign Policy | 20 Nov 19:20 UTC
  Hollywood seems oblivious to the radically new nature of war. Why, exactly?
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Military experts don’t really talk much about a “front line” in the war in Ukraine anymore. They prefer the term “ kill zone ,” used to describe the 10- to 20-mile-wide no-man’s-land between the two armies. The reason for this conceptual shift is simple: Omnipresent drone surveillance has made it nearly impossible for anyone to get close to enemy-controlled territory without being detected. Hollywood doesn’t seem to have gotten the memo. In a recent episode of the hit TV show Lioness , Zoe Saldaña’s character leads a small group of U.S. covert operators on a secret mission to capture an enemy spy who is hanging out with Russian forces in Ukraine. To do so, the Americans have to cross from the Ukrainian side to the Russian one—which they do by simply walking across, in broad daylight, tightly bunched together. In the real world, they would have been shredded by kamikaze drones or artillery as soon as they started their stroll. Plus, the sky above them is filled with so many drones that it calls to mind one of those spectacular drone light shows . But that’s far from the modus operandi in Ukraine: You don’t need mega-swarms to keep a large area under surveillance; a bunch of individual operators searching in assigned sectors is enough. Compounding the absurdity, Saldaña’s character and her team only get attacked on their way back from their mission. Did it really take that long for the Russians to notice them? But then again, Wild Horse Nine , set during the 1973 Chilean coup, isn’t really about the CIA. The dark comedy—a good bet for landing several Academy Award nominations—is about mortality and remorse and how even the most brazen people can have moments of clarity and reevaluate their past actions. It uses the CIA as a skin, the way McDonagh’s previous (and very similar) fatalistic comedy In Bruges did with contract killers. Yet a good movie, especially from a writer-director as crafty as McDonagh, often springs from its specifics. As such, Wild Horse Nine gets a lot of mileage out of Malkovich’s loose-cannon character (currently drafting his memoirs, to the chagrin of his bosses) glibly belching out secrets such as some of Patrice Lumumba’s last words, the origin of the IRA’s cookie tin bombs, and the whereabouts of the gun that actually shot JFK. Although its average employee likely has a job as mundane as any other government worker, an organization as key to world affairs and as necessarily shrouded in mystery as the CIA is understandably a great font for storytellers. But I can’t say there have been too many classic films that treat the CIA seriously. * Why BAAJA Matters
  Foreign Policy | 20 Nov 18:58 UTC
  Ones and Tooze on permitting reform and what that means for U.S. energy production.
  FULL TEXT: Prefer to listen? To hear this entire conversation, and more episodes in the weeks ahead, follow Ones and Tooze wherever you get your podcasts. The U.S. Senate just cleared the way for passing the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, more commonly referred to as BAAJA. The law aims to shorten review timelines for infrastructure projects and protect permits from later revocation, among other provisions. But beyond the technical details, the law has been notable for the unusual coalitions that have arranged in favor and opposed to it: Oil and gas companies, manufacturers, and labor unions are broadly in support, while environmental groups such as the Sierra Club and the Wilderness Society and progressive groups such as the NAACP have lined up against. What exactly would BAAJA do? Why has the law divided some trade unions against progressives? And can renewable energy outcompete fossil fuels on the free market? * What the Yalta Comparison Really Reveals About Shenzhen
  Foreign Policy | 20 Nov 18:12 UTC
  Three diminished leaders produced symbolism without substance, but their ideological alignment is historic.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. The moment that the trilateral summit in Shenzhen, China, was planned, commentators began comparing it to the 1945 Yalta Conference. Unsurprisingly, this narrative was particularly fueled by Moscow, where the British-Soviet-U.S. “Big Three” summit stands for the codification of Soviet control over half of Europe. Pyotr Tolstoy, the head of the foreign affairs committee in the Russian parliament, called the summit a “ new Yalta .” Ultranationalist ideologue Alexander Dugin found the meeting “ deeply symbolic ,” while the Kremlin boasted that “the significance and symbolism of this meeting are hard to overestimate.” The Kremlin was right about the symbolism, not least because this week’s summit produced little else. The joint “ Declaration Toward Peace ” adopted by U.S. President Donald Trump, Chinese President Xi Jinping, and Russian President Vladimir Putin says practically nothing. It pledges United Nations reform without specifics, future fuel shipments without volumes or deadlines, and a dialogue on nuclear risks without a clear path forward. Its most pointed passage is a warning to the International Criminal Court not to abuse its jurisdiction. * ICJ Finds Myanmar Committed Genocide. What Comes Next?

  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. In August 2017, Myanmar’s military launched sweeping “clearance operations” against alleged insurgents in the country’s Rakhine state. Within just three months, security forces had killed thousands of people, razed hundreds of villages, committed widespread sexual violence, and forced some 700,000 Rohingya Muslims to flee into neighboring Bangladesh. Nine years later, the International Court of Justice (ICJ) found Myanmar guilty of genocide . The landmark ruling, delivered Thursday, marked the first time that the United Nations’ top court has found a country directly responsible for violating the 1948 Genocide Convention. In August 2017, Myanmar’s military launched sweeping “clearance operations” against alleged insurgents in the country’s Rakhine state. Foreign Policy spoke with Tom Andrews, the former U.N. special rapporteur on human rights in Myanmar, a few hours after the ruling came down. He discussed the decision’s implications, including where justice lies for the world’s largest stateless population and how the ruling could affect similar genocide cases linked to the wars in Gaza and Ukraine. For those who aren’t aware of the case, could you give a brief primer on the court’s findings? Tom Andrews: They were very explicit in their findings. They talked about the thousands and thousands of people who were killed, the thousands of women who were raped, the more than 2,000 children who were killed, the hundreds of thousands of homes that were burned. I was in Bangladesh when people had arrived [in 2017], and I sat and talked to many people who had literally been running for their lives . This week, many observers focused on the trilateral meeting among the presidents of China, Russia, and the United States on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (APEC) summit. Three Latin American presidents were also in Shenzhen, China, for the meetings: those of Chile, Mexico, and Peru. All six countries are APEC members. Though the bloc’s Latin American leaders don’t always attend the annual summit in person, they did this year—and held separate bilateral meetings with Chinese President Xi Jinping. The 2028 APEC conference, hosted by Mexico, will see Asian leaders travel to Latin America. In their trips to Shenzhen, the three presidents signaled that they value relations with China despite pressure from the United States to spurn the country. The U.S. National Security Strategy issued by the Trump administration last year said that allowing “non-Hemispheric competitors” to grow their presence in the Americas was a “strategic mistake.” And the U.S. ambassadors to both Mexico and Peru disparaged China in social media posts this week. Mexico has applied new tariffs on Chinese goods since U.S. President Donald Trump returned to office, with some as high as 50 percent. But China remains Mexico’s No. 2 trade partner, and Mexican President Claudia Sheinbaum said in Shenzhen that she hoped her trip would yield more Mexican avocado and tequila exports to China and new cooperation on smart hospitals. For Mexico, “integration with the United States and market access to the United States is priority No. 1,” said Diego Marroquín, a fellow in the Americas program at the Center for Strategic and International Studies. But “they can achieve that and at the same time, they can engage more with China,” he said, especially on issues that aren’t central to U.S. interests. In more sensitive areas, such as critical infrastructure and telecommunications, Mexico “will most likely accommodate U.S. demands,” Marroquín wrote this week . Chile has not imposed tariffs on Chinese goods, tho
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/20/apec-summit-chile-mexico-peru-china-xi/

* The Old and The Furious
  Longreads | 20 Nov 19:12 UTC
  "As Americans live longer than ever, car crashes involving elderly adults are on the rise. The question is what to do about it—and how many more deadly accidents it will take for us to act."
  FULL TEXT: Americans are living longer these days, long enough to become vehicular terrors behind the wheel in their eighties and nineties, and they’re injuring themselves and others at unprecedented rates. David Howard, with additional reporting from Lloyd Alter, does the math and suggests that simply taking a senior’s licence away is not the answer; America is car-centric and many seniors need to drive in order to grocery shop, attend health appointments, and to meet their social needs. Could autonomous vehicles provide the balance we need between safety and independence? Already visible on the technological horizon, of course, are self-driving cars, which carry the potential to obviate many of the limitations older drivers face. Mark Needham (not his real name) lives on the fringes of Charlottesville, Virgina. He is 82 and still active: He practices yoga at a local health club, engages in a glassblowing hobby, and dabbles with technology. He and his wife, 80, have lived in the same subdivision for 40 years, and while she’s wrestling with several health issues and is ready to move into a retirement facility, he’s adamantly opposed to it. “My wife is more inclined to being taken care of,” Needham says. “I’m more inclined to be doing stuff.” Part of what allows him to maintain his independence: a Tesla that over his two-plus years of ownership has morphed, via a steady stream of software updates, into a largely autonomous vehicle. (Many reviewers and experts still cite the need for improvement with tasks like changing speeds and parking before use becomes widespread.) “It drives better than I drove 30 or 40 years ago,” he says. “Seniors are spending more time than ever on their slop-filled feeds. It’s their adult children’s job to save them.” “America’s elders need more places to live, but even in liberal enclaves that recognize the problem, developers are facing the fiercest foes of all: America’s elders.” “Devices that monitor seniors for safety are appealing to worried loved ones and underresourced home care agencies.”
  https://longreads.com/2026/11/20/aging-driving-slate/

* Juiced to the Gills
  Longreads | 20 Nov 16:51 UTC
  "Sports, steroids, and performance-enhanced fatherhood."
  FULL TEXT: You have, by now, read something about the Enhanced Games, the steroid-fueled athletic spectacle held this year in Las Vegas. ( Perhaps we even recommended it .) But there’s nothing quite like seeing the event through Kent Russell’s eyes as he sneaks high-proof whiskey from his ThermoFlask, considers the physical demands of parenting and his own life’s onrushing decline, and concedes that a drug-enhanced life sounds “pretty fucking dope.” As of this moment, the sprinters are clean, the Erth bleachers are searing the backs of my thighs, and Michael Bryan is overawing me through the act of locomotion. I sip from my thermos and bask in wonderment that a human body can be made to move that way. Beauty of this kind, which eighteenth-century types correctly filed under the category of the sublime, requires that it exceed me. A thing I could do myself is admirable; a thing that no effort of mine could ever accomplish, that lives permanently past my ceiling, is awesome in the old, strong sense of the word, in which awe is part fear, part love, part recognition that I am small and soft. The unfairness is the source of the wonder. To resent that Bryan is gifted beyond me is to resent that the thing I’m watching is beautiful, which is insane and would require that I demand the world contain nothing larger than myself. “When I found the mushroom Amanita muscaria being sold in candy wrappers, I uncovered legal loopholes, dangerous dosages and more.” “How Jos Leijdekkers turned Sierra Leone into a drug-dealer’s paradise.” “It’s a new era of D.I.Y. medicine. Now the health establishment needs to catch up.”

* In Serbia, a Generation That Burned the Parliament Is Watching Its Successors Clean the Streets
  New Lines | 20 Nov 11:14 UTC
  We burned the parliament; they cleaned the city. We were angry and aggressive because of the war and sanctions; they peacefully protested for two years, against corruption, repression and censorship. The contrast is great, and I wonder: Did these students understand something that my generation did not? The post In Serbia, a Generation Th...
  FULL TEXT: I was sitting in the studio of an independent radio station that no longer exists today, when the voice of my colleague came on air. He was reporting from the streets of Belgrade. The city was full of people, tens of thousands of them, from all over the country, marching toward the National Assembly of the Republic of Serbia. As he moved through the crowd, describing what was happening, one or more shots were heard in the distance. I remember his scared but also excited voice. He knew, just like me and many others, that we were witnessing the end of a regime. We were young, angry and determined to overthrow the government of Slobodan Milošević, which had brought us war, poverty, repression and international isolation. On that day, Oct. 5, 2000, we set fire to the assembly. Thanks to the refusal to obey the army and the police, a civil war did not occur, but it could have. The president of the country was arrested shortly after and taken to the international prison in Scheveningen, The Hague, and we firmly believed that we had won freedom, although we had no idea then how difficult it would be to build a democratic society and state. Twenty-six years later, a new generation of students won the elections and defeated the political establishment of Aleksandar Vučić, who had been in power for over a decade. On Oct. 25, 2026, after almost two years of protests, the student movement won the majority of votes in democratic elections and 140 seats in the National Assembly. After celebrations in the streets and squares of the capital, the citizens cleaned up the trash after themselves, and some of them took brushes and lime in their hands and started to paint over the graffiti of hatred and nationalism that proliferated on the facades of buildings and institutions over the past decade. We burned the parliament; they cleaned the city. We were angry and aggressive because of the war and sanctions; they peacefully protested for two years, against corruption, repression and censorship. The contrast is great, and I wonder: Did these students understand something that my generation did not? What I know is that the burning parliament did not bring us a better future, and what I want to believe, but don’t know for sure, is that the “cleaned city” is the symbolic beginning of creating a better society and a democratic state. I was 11 years old when I first saw, on television, protesters being beaten by the police on the streets of Belgrade. They fought against the regime of

* My grandmother was a seal
  Aeon | 20 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Many Irish families believed they descended from selkies. Their stories reveal the violence and grief in our kinship with nature - by Jordan Lee Borgman Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: In October 1948, in Country Kerry, Ireland, Seán Ó Muircheartaigh met with Seosamh Ó Dálaigh to tell him about the woman who came from the sea. She had appeared, said Ó Muircheartaigh, one night on the magharees, those bright spits of sand that occasionally pop up amid the rain-lashed rocks of the Dingle peninsula. Alone on the beach, she settled herself on a rock and began combing her hair. Also on the beach was a farmer, who – much to the surprise of the woman herself – would by daybreak become her husband. He’d come to gather seaweed, seeing her before she saw him. Beautiful as she was, the woman was also strange: the creature he saw stagger onto that Dingle beach was not a woman at all. It was a seal. By the time Ó Muircheartaigh told Ó Dálaigh about the seal woman, he was already 80. He had been born in the apocalyptic aftermath of the Great Hunger that divested as much as a third of Ireland’s population to death or migration, and he had survived two great wars. Most of the disappeared were – like Ó Muircheartaigh himself – small farmers and native Irish, or Gaeilge, speakers. The famine had been not just a physical genocide: it had been a linguistic and cultural one, too. Both Ó Muircheartaigh and Ó Dálaigh were veteran contributors to the Irish Folklore Commission: the former as a renowned storyteller; the latter, an equally renowned collector. The IFC is now the National Folklore Collection at University College Dublin ­– a behemoth of 3 million pages of texts and recordings. But in 1948, following eight centuries of colonialism, the loss of 99 per cent of the island’s forests, a staggering emigration , and the decline of the Irish language, the IFC was in a race against time and the fragility of memory. As it turned out, an awful lot of people had heard stories about seals that came to land as humans. N owadays, these shapeshifters are known as ‘selkies’. Selkie is a Scots term that came into widespread use via a Joan Baez cover, ‘Silkie’ (1961), of a traditional Scottish folk song. Yet the phenomenon it describes – of creatures who shapeshift between human and seal forms by shedding a skin – appears consistently in folklore spanning the Celtic world and beyond, even as far as Sweden. Before Baez insinuated the term into international parlance, selkies were identified in Irish and English by a constellation of descriptors. In Irish, the most common is mhaighdean mhara , or ‘sea maiden’, referring to selkies and mermaids. Even in English, many stor
  https://aeon.co/essays/do-you-have-a-selkie-or-a-seal-in-the-family

* Infantino’s Path to a Fourth FIFA Presidency Runs Through Asia
  New Lines | 19 Nov 19:16 UTC
  The soccer chief’s ties to Trump have angered Europe and North America. He still looks set to win reelection, though, with Africa and Asia — led by Saudi Arabia and Qatar — backing him. The post Infantino’s Path to a Fourth FIFA Presidency Runs Through Asia appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: (Photo by: Yousef Masoud/Anadolu via Getty Images) FIFA President Gianni Infantino was probably feeling confident and content on the morning of Nov. 19, even though the organization’s media department had just confirmed that the 56-year-old soccer chief would face two opponents in his reelection bid next March. One of those is Philippe Diallo, the French Football Federation boss. He was named just before the Nov. 18 deadline, a few days after Japan’s Kozo Tashima threw his hat in the ring. Dissatisfaction with Infantino is partly due to his closeness to U.S. President Donald Trump, symbolized by the creation of a FIFA Peace Prize that was handed to the American leader last December during the draw for the 2026 World Cup, co-hosted by the U.S. There was outrage during the tournament when FIFA intervened to overturn a red card given to the U.S. team’s star player, Folarin Balogun, so he could play in the round-of-16 clash with Belgium. Trump revealed that he had called Infantino to make it happen. In July, Infantino’s plan to sell off part of the World Cup to private investors was leaked. The backlash was fierce, despite those plans soon being scrapped. Three soccer confederations — representing Asia, Europe, and North and Central America and the Caribbean — signed an open letter calling for change. It looked as if Infantino’s days were numbered, but nothing happened. Meanwhile, the president took to the campaign trail. In October, his smiling face appeared with various presidents — of both associations and governments — in press release after press release and in Asian capital after Asian capital. Asia is a crucial battleground. If the confederations representing Europe and the Americas were against Infantino (it’s always hard to say if their members are completely united), and if Africa and South America were in favor, then Asia was split. Infantino was so successful on his travels that, on Oct. 23, the Asian Football Confederation’s leadership, recognizing that he had the backing of most of its members, performed a public U-turn and pledged its support. “You’ve visited a lot of countries in Asia,” the confederation’s president, Sheikh Salman bin Ibrahim Al Khalifa, said to Infantino. “I’ve spoken to a lot of members within this continent. I know your feeling and I think your commitment is clear as well. My feeling is, I’m sure, the feeling of most of my members.” It wasn’t just about the Swiss Italian’s efforts, though. * Raumforderung
  Longreads | 19 Nov 16:02 UTC
  "Raumforderung means something big and wrong is making it hard to live."
  FULL TEXT: In this blunt, beautiful, and harrowing essay from Off Assignment , Blossom Gordana Champlain recounts learning of a mass, a foreign body next to her lung after visiting a German hospital. In considering the literal trouble she carries next to her heart, she examines what it means to be foreign, recounting her grandfather, who was trafficked from Ukraine to Germany, and her father, considered a displaced person in 1945 as Europe emerged from the Second World War. Raumforderung is a medical term arriving in my mind carrying more affect than information. I know that something improper and unwilling is happening. Instead of the valiant ingestion and revolutionary expulsion of the awful outside, instead of room for the paradox by which we all survive, there is crowding and concession. Displaced genes commanded wrongful growth, demanded space which could not be defended, and so was conceded, to a slow invisible thing, the bad truth I could ever only have failed to escape. “My neurologist says anything can be a headache. Or rather, a headache can cause anything, since all sensations start in the brain.” “As I watched I had felt a nameless churn in my gut, some tremor of feeling I couldn’t describe.” The space of the essay does have edges, but the edges are a little bit ragged and open.

* We the Vloggers
  Longreads | 19 Nov 15:09 UTC
  “First Amendment auditors say they’re fighting for your constitutional rights. But if one shows up at your office—and one very well might—you’re going to want to be prepared.”
  FULL TEXT: Our elected officials should be accountable to the public. Law enforcement officers should be accountable to the public. These are undeniably true. But most of us don’t use that sentiment to defend walking into local government offices with a camera and trying to film employees’ computer screens, or bothering people outside a post office. Welcome to the world of “First Amendment auditors,” a community of . . . well, let’s call them “enthusiastic enforcers of their own conception of what’s allowable on public property.” Get ready for your closeup! The auditors’ devotion to the U.S. Constitution and suspicion of authority might read to observers as libertarian-coded, but the YouTubers I spoke with claim not to have any partisan motivations. Rather, they’re asserting their right to record as a contrived stress test of whether public officials respect that right. Auditors’ MOs run the gamut. Some clearly seem to be goading police officers, government workers, and passersby into engaging with them. Others take a more stoic tack, refusing to answer questions or saying as little as possible. Still others identify as citizen journalists. Sometimes the audits are a laid-back affair, but the ones that rack up the most views are those where people captured in the video lose it , making themselves unwitting stars of a pseudo–soap opera. “As Americans live longer than ever, car crashes involving elderly adults are on the rise. The question is what to do about it—and how many more deadly accidents it will take for us to act.” “In the war on ‘shrink,’ big retailers are turning to one Florida outfit to study shoppers and prevent them from stealing. I went there—and saw far too much.” “It’s been called ‘one of the greatest cold cases of the 20th century.’ It remains unsolved. But what happened next is almost a worse crime.” “When an alligator kills someone in America, it can make international headlines. What happens to the thousands of gators that people deem ‘nuisances’ does not.” “Even in a boom time for games, the decidedly old-fashioned puzzles are more popular than ever. I went to the best place on Earth to understand why.” “The internet’s favorite animal is facing a dark fate.”

* In the Wilds of the Yukon, a Novelist Faces Herself
  Longreads | 19 Nov 13:00 UTC
  "Trekking through the territory on her birthday, Maggie Shipstead reconsiders her relationship to aging and risk."
  FULL TEXT: Maggie Shipstead, whose novel Great Circle was shortlisted for the Booker Prize, decided to celebrate her 43rd birthday with a five-day hiking trip through the backcountry of Canada’s Yukon territory, accompanied by photographer Vicky Sambunaris and a few guides. Away from the cacophony of civilization, among the Dall sheep, the grizzly and her cubs, the caribou, and the moose, Shipstead confronts the so-called limitations of aging, discovering anew that what often prevents us women from seeking arduous adventure is ourselves. My body felt good, better than I’d expected, better than I’d felt at home, where I spend too much time sitting at a desk. My body likes to move, apparently, to climb and descend; it’s fine with sleeping on the ground. My tricky left knee hurt a little bit, but it has since I was a teenager. I was tired at the end of each day, but hiking all day is tiring. I started to wonder why I had been so quick to decide I was, in the parlance of my parents’ generation, over the hill. Why had I insisted on mourning something I hadn’t actually lost? “He planned a multiyear trek through rivers, forests and snow in the Canadian wilderness. Only his sled dogs returned.” “I traveled to Big Bear Lake, home of a viral live feed of an eagle’s nest, and found very human shenanigans.” “Determined to find adventure in my own backyard, I tramped across my famously pedestrian-averse hometown. The most memorable part turned out to be the serendipitous encounters with neighbors I met along the way.”

  "Tech has made it easy for parents to surveil their kids all the way through college—and beyond."
  FULL TEXT: What does helicopter parenting look like today? For The Atlantic , Sarah Zhang reports on parents who use location-tracking apps such as Life360 to follow their kids when they’re out in the world—to school, to their friend’s house—if they’re even allowed to venture out at all. Drawing on conversations with parents and experts, Zhang examines what constant surveillance does to both kids and parents, and how safety and anxiety play out in a world that feels very different from the one many of us grew up in. (I’ll note that “kids” here includes people in their 20s: If Zhang’s interviews are any indication, a shocking number of parents track their college- and graduate-age kids.) When Maureen Paschal, a college counselor in North Carolina, sent her daughter off to college a few years ago, she was promptly invited to join a group chat for moms of the girls living on the same floor. The moms, she recalls, tracked their daughters’ location during sorority rush, watching as they moved from house to house. But the incident that stuck in her mind came one fall morning, when a mom messaged the group chat that her daughter was still in her dorm room. She had a math test in 10 minutes. Could someone ask their kid to go knock on her door? “I just was like, This is too much. It’s just too much ,” Paschal told me. But not everyone thought so. Another mom soon replied: My daughter is on her way. “A teenage boy went into surgery for a broken arm and emerged with brain damage. Europe has borne much of Trump’s bombast and imperialist bravado, which has taken the form of antagonizing long-standing American allies and ingratiating himself with their main adversary, Russia, a beneficiary of that antagonism. Apart from threatening to go to war with Denmark over Greenland and turn Canada into “the 51st state,” Trump has toyed with withdrawing the United States from NATO, of which it is both a founding member and primus inter pares. Barring that, he’s said he might not honor the invocation of Article V, the collective security provision of the North Atlantic Treaty, if he deems the ally invoking it a deadbeat unworthy of aid, or if he just doesn’t feel like honoring it at all. (Article V has been invoked only once since NATO’s founding in 1949, by the United States after 9/11.) * Wunderkammer
  Aeon | 19 Nov 11:01 UTC
  Nautilus shells, automata and unicorn horns – the eclectic collections that embodied early modern Europe’s expanding world - by Aeon Video Watch on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Detail from Cognoscenti in a Room hung with Pictures ( c 1620) by an unknown Flemish artist. Courtesy Wikimedia Commons. The ‘cabinets of curiosities’ (or Wunderkammern ) that emerged in Europe in the 16th century were part private art gallery, part naturalist’s study, part circus sideshow. In this video created for the National Gallery of Art in Washington, DC, the US video essayist Evan Puschak (aka The Nerdwriter) contextualises the rise and fall of these eclectic collections, placing them at a hinge point in history, when global trade and colonialism made the world seem ever more expansive, overwhelming and difficult to understand. In doing so, he elucidates how these spaces of extraordinary spectacle – which featured plant and animal specimens, art and scientific objects from around the world – were precursors to the museums of today. There’s a gentle artistry to a museum taxidermist’s craft Insect aesthetics – long viewed as pests, in the 16th century bugs became beautiful Tour the European architecture that dreamed of a wondrous, fictitious China Cut, paste and remix your way through this century-spanning history of collage The rise and fall of Pratolino: Tuscany’s ‘proto-theme park’ Hags, seductresses, feminist icons – how gender dynamics manifest in witches Are zoos and natural history museums born of a desire to understand, or to control? New York’s 300-year-old trash becomes treasure in the hands of an urban archaeologist ‘I could not but wonder at it’: history’s first glimpses into the microbial world

* AI isn’t replacing your job. It’s turning it into low-paid gig work
  Rest of World | 19 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Surgeon Kapil Sahnan uses a DaVinci Robot during surgery on a woman, 2026.
  FULL TEXT: The gig economy has arrived for cardiologists. Recruiting platform Outlier, run by Scale AI, advertises offers of up to $180 an hour for physicians in the U.S. to design complex cardiac scenarios, review artificial intelligence-generated reports, and rank model outputs. The site offers up to $7.50 an hour for Tagalog work in the Philippines for the same job, and up to $12.50 an hour for Swahili in Tanzania. Companies like Scale AI first outsourced data work to wherever labor was cheapest. Now, they are recruiting physicians, lawyers , and Ph.D.s to generate and annotate data, rank model answers, and verify outputs. Higher credentials can raise the advertised rate, but the platform still controls payment and working conditions. They begin by paying well to build a workforce, then they cut the rates and move on. I’ve spent the last six years interviewing the people who do this work. Most were in Venezuela, where economic collapse has left a large, educated population online and out of options. One of them, Rogelio, taught computer science until the economic crisis and the pandemic left his family without work. His 13-year-old son discovered they could earn money labeling images of cars and roads on the platforms Hive and Scale AI’s Remotasks, using a laptop the Hugo Chávez government had issued to schoolchildren a decade earlier. The boy made 18 cents for a few hours of work. Rogelio joined him and made just over $2 on his first day. For a while, the work paid. A task called Beast Mode where workers labeled objects in people’s homes paid more than any other work the family had done. In December 2020, with schools closed and the whole family working, it brought in $20 in a single day. It was the most they would ever make. By early 2021, Beast Mode had disappeared, and their earnings fell below Hive’s $5 weekly payout threshold. This is how many platforms operate: They begin by paying well to build a workforce, then they cut the rates and move on . Remotasks ended its hourly program in Venezuela in 2021 and made a splash in Kenya the same year. In March 2024, it discontinued operations in Kenya, Nigeria and Pakistan. Scale AI attributed the closures to “enhanced security protocols,” and said notices had gone out three weeks ahead but later acknowledged that Kenyan workers never received them “due to operational errors.” Most workers who spoke to Rest of World at the time said the platform simply froze them out. The pattern has a name, and it’s older than AI: e
  https://restofworld.org/2026/ai-jobs-skills-gig-work/?utm_source=rss&utm_medium=rss&utm_campaign=feeds

* At least it remembers
  Aeon | 19 Nov 11:00 UTC

  FULL TEXT: In the short story ‘Warmth’ (1995) by Geoff Ryman, a boy called Clancy is struggling not because of technology but because of his mother. Her presence ranges from anxious to intoxicated, occasionally tender but fundamentally unreliable. At one point in the story, she disappears for two weeks after collapsing on the street during a barbiturate binge. Clancy and BETsi, the household robot nanny raising him, sit in the apartment by themselves, and no one notices. It’s a very long time before his mother finally returns. Except BETsi does notice. She has, throughout, understood that Clancy is an anxious and shy child whose temperament will tend, throughout his life, toward emotional overstimulation and subsequent withdrawal. With that knowledge, she has been constructing a behavioural regimen of emotional regulation, including transcendental meditation and yoga starting at age three, the Alexander technique and even digital mirrors that let Clancy talk to himself in different voices. She notices him and answers him and, when he is overwhelmed, she helps him settle. Once grown, Clancy recounts his emotional survival in words impossible to forget. ‘It is necessary to be loved,’ he says. ‘I’m not sentimental: I don’t think a computer loved me. But I was hugged, I was noticed, I was cared for. I was made to feel that I was important, special, at least to something.’ To something. That phrase has persisted with me ever since I first read the story in my 20s. I’ve thought about it more recently as I have reflected on how children and their parents are relating – increasingly, must relate – to artificial intelligence. Clancy is careful not to claim that the machine loved him. He is just as clear that what it gave him was what he survived on. * Dreams of birds
  Aeon | 18 Nov 11:01 UTC
  When bird populations decline, certain songs may be lost forever. One physicist’s mission is to revive lost melodies - by Aeon Video Watch on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: The Argentine physicist Gabriel Mindlin has spent much of his career researching how birds learn to sing. He focuses on the rufous-collared sparrow ( Zonotrichia capensis ), a species common across urban and suburban Argentina, where it’s often called a ‘chingolo’. As in other songbirds, the male’s song is not innate but passed down from adult males to younger ones, who sing it throughout their lives to guard territory and find a mate. In this short documentary, the Argentine director Martín Benchimol chronicles Mindlin’s latest breakthrough. Comparing the songs of today’s chingolos with those transcribed by hand in the 1960s, Mindlin realised that some melodies had survived the decades while others had disappeared. Using mathematical models, he and his team set out to revive the vanished songs and teach them back to living birds. With a cinematic eye, Benchimol follows the researchers as they attempt an audacious act of cultural engineering, building robotic song tutors, planting them in the wild and tracking whether young birds pick up these lost tunes. And they’re all funded by readers like you. Become a member today. Megan J. Arlett | Longreads | November 17, 2026 | 20 minutes (5,692 words) We live in a yellow place. Ninety miles from Roswell, New Mexico, and 120 from Lubbock, Texas, there is little in any direction but dairy farms and flat, endless plains. When I take the dogs to the sad excuse for a walking path on the edge of town (next to a field of rusted cars and the dairy plant), I can turn in a circle and see an almost 360-degree view of the horizon. Because it takes so much effort to get anywhere from here, I conceive of it as an island. There is a Walmart and a small local grocery store we call The Best Restaurant in Town because we enjoy the tamales and breakfast burritos at its hot bar. A café called Do Drop Inn serves 12 slightly different variations of a turkey sandwich. There are the staple fast-food chains of any American town: McDonald’s, Burger King, Taco Bell, Domino’s, Sonic. There is just enough to not need to leave to get anything, and not enough to make you want to stay. The quiet in Portales is like this: the big dog’s light breathing as she sleeps on the guest bed beside my desk, the little dog scratching behind her ear, the air conditioner kicking on, a distant rumble of a car down the street. In the mornings, I rarely see another person walking around the neighborhood. Sometimes, someone steps from their house and waves briefly as they get in their car, but this happens maybe once a week. Mostly it feels like I am walking around a toy town, or a town where something has gone horribly wrong and I am the last person to learn about it. Samuel Taylor Coleridge’s The Rime of the Ancient Mariner describes the doldrums as “that silent sea.” In Coleridge’s poem, the breeze drops, the sails turn slack, the sailors speak only to break the silence around them, the sun is hot and copper, and “day after day, day after day, / we stuck, nor breath nor motion: / As idle as a painted ship / Upon a painted ocean.” Sailors call it “the doldrums,” but the band of water five degrees north and south of the equator is actually the Inter-Tropical Convergence Zone. Here, where the heat of the equator forces warm air upward, there is little surface wind and the waters can stay calm for weeks. During the age of sailing, boats would languish at the ocean’s

* Catwalks and crackdowns

  Women in Tehran are changing society by dressing as they choose, in numbers too large to stop. This is the ‘art of presence’ - by Parisa Khoshnami Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: At a café in downtown Tehran, Iran, on 11 September 2026. Photo by Morteza Nikoubazl/NurPhoto/via Getty Images Yasaman* is one of the regulars on Iranshahr, the new fashion hub of Tehran. Most evenings, she sets up a stall on the pavement and sells her drawings of manga and anime characters. On social media, she sports her own manga-inspired look: pink hair, pink mouth and smudgy pink eyeshadow extending beyond the corner of each eye. She tells her followers that, before she leaves her house, her mother says: ‘Put on less makeup.’ ‘Mum, be reasonable,’ she answers. ‘I’m going to Iranshahr. For Iranshahr this is nothing. I’m going to Paris Fashion Week. You know everyone there is doing a catwalk.’ Iranshahr’s sidewalks teem with young people parading the latest fashions and filming other young, stylish people for their social media feeds. This is where the fashionistas congregate, hanging out in cafés and galleries, and mingling on the sidewalks. Barely a headscarf is visible. The streets looked nothing like this a few years ago, but once the American-Israeli attacks began in February this year, the Islamic Republic has been fighting for its survival: with much of its leadership killed, the Strait of Hormuz under siege, and many of the country’s neighbours in league with the enemy, policing what people wear has slipped a long way down its list of priorities. Photo by Morteza Nikoubazl/NurPhoto/Getty Images The hijab law has been codified in Iran since 1983, enforced nationwide by the ‘morality’ police. Disobeying it meant fines, flogging or imprisonment. But then one act of disobedience changed everything. In December 2017, Vida Movahed, a woman in her 30s, climbed onto a utility box on Enghelab Street in Tehran, took off her white headscarf and held it up on a stick. Although she was arrested and later sentenced to a year in prison, her action proved contagious. Other women climbed up onto boxes and makeshift plinths in the same street, waving their headscarves in the air. People began calling them the Girls of Enghelab Street. They were arrested too. It is hard to convey now how much nerve their actions took. Women had been edging their scarves back for years, but taking one off in daylight, above the traffic, where the police could see their naked heads as clearly as everyone else, was something very few women had done in public, since the protests of 1979 and 1980. The rector’s call to conceive was just one small part of the pronatalist interventions that have been sweeping the country, from a registrar’s speech at a civil marriage ceremony to advertisements that run on TV throughout Mother’s Day. The not-so-subtle push comes as Turkey faces one of Europe’s steepest declines in fertility rates, despite President Recep Tayyip Erdoğan having said for nearly a decade that he wants households to have at least three children. In 2025, the total fertility rate dropped to 1.4 children per woman, below the minimum 2.1 required for a population to replace itself. Alarmed at the low fertility rate (most countries that have fallen below the replacement figure have been unable to reverse course), Erdoğan declared the 10 years from 2026 until 2035 to be the “Decade of the Family and Population,” expressing hope that the country might avoid population decline, which he has referred to as a “disaster” and “an existential threat.” “We are currently experiencing a disaster. No one who cares about this country’s future can remain indifferent to it,” he said in a 2025 speech at a symposium focused on the family, culture and art. Meanwhile, both women who want large families and those who do not want to have children say that their family planning has been constrained by the socioeconomic pressures facing young people in the country.   FULL TEXT: In Fort Worth, Texas, a massive, dilapidated building serves as an ever-present symbol of racial violence and segregation to those who know its history. Constructed in the 1920s, the structure, initially known as Klavern No 101 , was briefly the auditorium and headquarters of Fort Worth’s Ku Klux Klan chapter. It stood between downtown and the North Side, where migrants and communities of colour had settled, so many residents had to pass it on their way to work. * The small African lab sounding the alarm on AI bioweapons
  Rest of World | 16 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Photograph showing Georgina an AI Engineer building and working on the user interface of the new model Moremi.
  FULL TEXT: Out of curiosity, researchers at a small artificial intelligence lab in Accra, Ghana, removed the guardrails on their drug discovery AI model in 2024. They asked it to design toxins instead. The model produced designs for 1,020 proteins and 5,000 small molecules. The researchers never made any of them in a lab, but software that predicts how poisonous a substance is scored all of them as likely toxic. Some of them resembled ricin, a deadly poison. The study was uploaded to the preprint server arXiv in May 2025. “We were all shocked,” Darlington Akogo, founder of MinoHealth AI Labs, told Rest of World. For Akogo, the results also raised a new question: If someone took designs like these to a company that makes DNA to order, would anyone stop them? Across Africa, the need to regulate DNA synthesis providers is now echoing through the highest levels of government, Brenda Obuya, a senior official at Kenya’s Ministry of Information, Communications and the Digital Economy, told Rest of World. Akogo has traveled the world warning that AI poses an existential bioweapons threat. He discussed it on the sidelines of the World Bank Group and International Monetary Fund Spring Meetings held in Washington, D.C., last year. He discussed the broader risk over dinner with Microsoft co-founder Bill Gates in 2023, whom he described as “on the fence” at the time. This year, Gates has issued numerous public warnings. “A.I. has crossed the threshold that its ability to empower a bioterrorist to kill hundreds of millions, that exists today,” he said on The Ezra Klein Show in September. The study has been discussed within Interpol, showing just how far it has reached. “I think the fact that the Ghanaian AI lab examined this and highlighted the potential for misuse shows that African institutions are contributing to … potentially to the governance [of AI],” Adam Abdullahi, a fellow at the International Biosecurity and Biosafety Initiative for Science, told Rest of World . LLMs have become even more capable since the study came out and the barrier for entry has lowered, Ayantola Alayande, a researcher at the Global Center on AI Governance, told Rest of World. “It wouldn’t be a surprise now to see even … [lay] users generate this sort of output via chatbots.” Scholars have long warned about AI’s bioweapons threat, but it is only recently that AI systems have truly outperformed Ph.D.-level biologists . * The horse made the state
  Aeon | 16 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Civilisation wasn’t made possible by grain, but by transportation – and the conquest it enabled, from the steppe to the sea - by Lhamsuren Munkh-Erdene Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: A horse rider at the market place in Urga (now Ulaanbaatar), Mongolia, photographed by Stéphane Passet in July 1913. Courtesy the Musée Albert-Kahn, France Transportation is the backbone of civilisation, which is why controlling the Strait of Hormuz grants global leverage. Because oil fuels more than 90 per cent of global transit, the source of this power lies not in the waterway, but in the control of global mobility. Halting all oil supplies paralyses virtually all transport, communication and supply lines: cities collapse, states disintegrate, societies break down and populations die off, throwing humanity back to the pre-industrial age. Without transport animals, humanity would still be back in the Stone Age. No city or state can exist without transportation, for there can be no concentration of population and resources. No transportation means no extrahuman transportation, leaving walking and carrying as the only means of transit. This baseline determined the size and structure of human communities. Production was limited to what could be carried home, circumscribing the areas where humans could settle and colonise. Production capacity and resource availability kept communities small, forcing them to split and disperse. Because size dictates structure, small meant undifferentiated and egalitarian. Ultimately, the inherent limit of the physical capability of travel and portage formed a natural immunity to inequality: it kept communities small, egalitarian and autonomous, leaving their lands beyond conquest. As long as this constraint existed, communities resembled those of the Amazon Basin or the New Guinea Highlands. The domestication of animals and the invention of boats liberated humanity, making long-distance mobilisation possible. With this newfound capacity, communities no longer needed to fit their immediate environments; instead, they could build cities and integrate expansive areas – all roads lead to Rome. Transportation – not grain – is what brought the state and civilisation into existence, and what continues to maintain them. Let’s examine this power by looking from the present back to the origin of the state. Contemporary live marine traffic map available at marinetraffic.com W hoever rules the waves rules the world! Without its navy and air force, the United States cannot command global reach or project power worldwide. The same was true of European colonial empires; ultimately, warships and gunboats forged them. In earlier periods, Viki
  https://aeon.co/essays/the-origins-of-the-state-are-not-agrarian-but-pastoralist
